In other words, no country may stand by the other country as far as liability is concerned. Secondly, The European Central Bank may not buy government bonds. The financial transaction tax would be an excellent instrument for this. I therefore expressly welcome the fact that the financial transaction tax is explicitly mentioned in this recently agreed work package. The modern Austrian tourist and guest is currently shorter, but also more often on holiday. He prefers to drive his own car from his residence to the hotel. However, it does not want to be affected at all or hardly affected by transport, and it does not want to experience the negative effects such as traffic noise or environmental pollution at all. I would just like to mention a few, for example, the project where about 20 communities in six Alpine countries are living under the umbrella organization. “Pears of the Alps” have joined forces. They have committed themselves particularly to ecological criteria and have also focused on sustainable mobility. They want to achieve that their holiday guests can travel as easily as possible by public transport, then they can also be mobile on site, they can move very sustainablely, and so to speak, do not miss their car at all during this holiday period. In front of the curtain would also be the project of Villa Vita in Pamhagen, which offers their holiday guests electric cars with which they can explore the surroundings, or of course also the gentle mobility project, the SAMO project, in Werfenweng, from which the holidaymakers and of course also the holiday destination benefit. Of course, the interest in these special mobility offers and especially in the ecological footprint of tourism is great, but we also need to be aware of it again and again: Finally, the convenience of the holiday guest will prevail. Therefore, packages must be chained, which are individually tailored to the holiday guest and which, above all, also offer very sustainable mobility, so that transport can really be discharged. You also need to know that during the planning phase, the guest wants to know exactly what is expected of him on the spot. It is therefore also necessary to invest in a good information campaign, so that information security can also be offered to travellers. My group supports and welcomes this motion for a resolution, since, of course, the development of a new mobility solution for the very sensitive tourist areas of Austria can also improve the quality of life and the quality of holiday.

ParlaMint-AT_2011-06-15-024-XXIV-NRSITZ-00109_d8e19058	Madam President. Federal Minister! Ladies and gentlemen, ladies and gentlemen! As the discussion on the 2010 tourism report has already shown, the tourism and leisure industry for Austria is an important factor, and we also know how important a clean, healthy environment is in this context and how decisive this factor can be for further positive development in this sector. The Committee has already discussed the fact that increasing road transport is not only a major challenge for individual transport, but also, of course, for the whole sector of leisure and tourism. There is therefore a need for a rethinking as a whole and measures with new approaches to sustainable, environmentally sound mobility. Many experiences from tourism and transport projects in recent years show that new solutions for soft mobility not only bring significant reductions in CO2 emissions, but often also benefits for the leisure and tourism industries. Ladies and gentlemen, ladies and gentlemen! Customer mobility is particularly important in tourism. It is not only about the subject of arrival or departure, it is more and more about mobility during the stay. Therefore, concepts, which are limited only to the field of arrival and departure, are no longer sufficient today, but here we are dealing with increasing mobility demands which we must also meet our guests in tourism. Mobility management is the key word to the outside. Mobility management begins in passenger transport, when deciding on the choice of transport, and aims to ensure effective, socially sound and environmentally friendly mobility with measures that essentially affect the fields of information, communication and coordination. It is therefore to be welcomed that the Ministry of Economic Affairs, as well as the BMVIT and the Ministry of Life, have already set up successful activities and implemented projects. In Salzburg, for example, there is a project on timetable and information systems “new” containing information on all public transport from international rail connections to local call collection axis, up to consulting programmes on mobility management for the tourism, leisure and youth sectors. Ladies and gentlemen, ladies and gentlemen! Our summer guests will receive a summer card free of charge from the third night. With this summer card you can visit all museums that use swimming pools, all lifts and public transport free of charge. You can imagine that this is a true success story. This, of course, costs relatively much money, and the communities are required to spend this money. The Bregenzerwald summer card is very much accepted. We have found that the number of nights in summer tourism is constantly rising. We have more satisfied guests and much less traffic. It is also an offer for the locals. You can buy a season ticket with which you can then also accept this offer very cost-effectively. Therefore, there is a need to create sustainable mobility offers – by linking leisure activities with the use of public transport – we need the expansion of cycle road networks, we want a meaningful optimization of the cycle road schedule, and there is also a need for electric mobility. There are also rental systems for e-bikes and e-cars. Our tourism industry is required to provide a high quality of the environment and stay so that our market opportunities increase. Let us look at these examples and try to implement them throughout the world!

Example: Weekend service: 48h a piece; if you have 2 weekends in a row, only in the same month is expected, I had e.g. two WEs in a row (end of May and early June) and thus exceeded the maximum weekly service time; Night service under the week (...) cannot be taken home after 24 hours but depends on eight hours.” He also writes: ‘NÖ: In most hospitals as a result of night shifts, average hourly average 70 as well as poor pay in case of high responsibility.” In the AKH there is still the famous “Hebamen Conflict: Another midwife in the AKH through burnout in the state of illness, this week a midwife provides 6 services (6x12,5h) and thus significantly exceeds the maximum hours, per ND receive the midwives 30 Euro gross, hourly wage gross below 3 €!!!” Moreover, employers do not even want to pay their employees properly. They just split working hours according to the motto: Working time is not full working time, working time may be working time and waiting time. This is an intolerable state. That can't be, and it doesn't have to be. We have long called for – and have already made a number of requests to do so – that it is time to be working full time and that there should be strict compliance with the maximum weekly working time in public hospitals. In no case will we agree to a deterioration in the current legal situation. Let us take a look at the Committee's position on the amendments to the working time directives introduced by the Coalition Parties. – This includes a splitting of working time in active and inactive willingness to work, with so-called non-active times not counting on the maximum level of working time, or no longer fully valid as working time. This includes an extension of the average weekly working time, which would lead to different charges of time off to weekly working hours. It also includes an extension of the calculation periods. I mean, before an Austrian politician or an Austrian social minister in Europe advocates social minimum standards, he should first and foremost ensure that they are applied and implemented in his own country. Everything else would be grotesque and absolutely unbelievable. We have relatively good, clear and clear social and labour laws in Austria. These perform a certain protection function, including in the field of maximum working time limits and calculation periods. These provisions are mandatory. You can't get out of it or opt out of it, as it's so beautiful in Neudeutsche. This rejection of the so-called opting out is natural to all of us in Austria. Thus, our laws prohibit any possibility for the individual abandonment of workers to this protective function of the Working Time Directive, the so-called Opting-Out, of the permitted maximum working time. We reject the opting out completely from the Freedom Party – but hopefully you too – at least as far as Austria is concerned. The Social Democratic SPE Group also rejects it. And the European Trade Union Confederation also rejects it. However, in the Austrian hospitals, employees are required to accept employment contracts, which include exactly the opposite of what has been decided there. They are required to agree to opt-out contracts, although Austria does not even use the opt-out and even fight it officially. The result is then to work 70 hours a week or 50 hours a piece, with the above pocket player tricks, that you don't even count your working time full as working time. This is always justified by the fact that a law-compliant solution would not be necessary to finance it. This is what Mr Bartenstein has told me in the Committee. I think that's nonsense because there's money. It's just wondering what it's spent on. There is no doubt that you, Minister Hundstorfer, have to bear the responsibility that the necessary resources are available, that you, Minister Hundstorfer, must also ensure that the laws are complied with, and that you, Minister Hundstorfer, must also ensure that there is no exploitation of Austrian workers who are de facto required to accept opt-out contracts and conditions, just because the Austrian Federal Government lacks the necessary money, because, as we all know and have heard today, it flows in bank rescue packages or in Pleite States in the south. Our money for our people! – That's our motto, and that's not ideological or hetceric, and it's not even plausative at all, but it's simple and just pragmatic. Since in this context it is always said that we want to advance the ‘social Europe’, i.e. uniform social standards for the whole of the EU, I do not want to mention that unified European standards only make sense if there is a single European economic and fiscal area if there is a single European central state. There's no such thing as that. We don't want that either! We want good laws for our country, which we can be sovereign and responsible for. And what is good and useful for our country, this may be quite different for other economies. Just so that we can understand each other correctly: I am not indifferent to the Portuguese or Estonian RTAs and nurses, but I am an Austrian politician and think that we should first take care of the Austrians. [[Congratulations at the FPÖ.]] However, where Europe can get involved is where people are actually exploited. First study: Doping for workers is no longer rare. Performance-enhancing agents such as psycho and neuropharmaceuticals are increasingly being used to cope with stress and occupational stress. The study continues to say that this leads to an artificial increase in performance in the short term, but after a short time it will become so to speak and the performance curve will go down significantly again. The only proper compensation for overload would be a better work-life balance. But, according to the study, it is not possible to do this precisely because of the longer and longer working hours. Ladies and gentlemen! Apparently, more and more people in Austria need medication in order to cope with stress at work at all, and with it they are gradually ruining themselves. But for you this is obviously not a problem – at least not a problem enough to look at it. For you, it is not a problem enough to reflect on the reduction of working time. Next study: Nine out of ten respondents feel horrified at work. The time pressure that prevails there is the biggest load factor at all. 32 percent of all employees in Austria feel heavy mental stress at work. At least once a year, 80 percent of all workers feel that they can no longer cope with their lives because of severe stress. The result is a marked increase in mental illness due to workplace stress. The pressure at the Austrian workplace is increasing, resulting in mental illness. But for you, that's not a subject, you don't look at it, because you don't want to think about shortening working hours. Mr Riepl says we have almost paradisiacal conditions here in Austria. I mean, we're far away from that. There's another study that proves this. In this study, it is worth reading that three quarters of all self-employed persons who regularly overtime work are ready would be or would strive to work less, work shorter, and be willing to take a clear wage-off for that. They experience the burdens, both physical and psychological, as well as such intense that they are willing to give up part of their income. Ladies and gentlemen, there are more and more people who say: That's not how we want to continue, which it's too much. And how do you react? – For you, the reduction of working time is not a matter. I could tell you for the first time that from the seventh, at the latest eight-day working hours, the risk of an accident at work has increased significantly and that from the seventh hour of work, the probability of a mid-term occupational disease is increasing. With you, however, all material arguments are sustainable. They don't look – we Greens are already doing this! We do not want growth at all costs, especially at the price of increasing burdens on workers. We want to get away from the paradigm: in any case, economic growth, definitely an increase in profits! We want people to have a good life. We want a better work-life balance. We do not want 80 percent of all employees to be at least once a year before a collapse. We don't want that! [[Congratulation with the greens.]] We Greens also have proposals where we need to: We have to get away from the overtime! The overtime and the extra hours of part-time workers must be more expensive – otherwise it will not go! We also want to get away from the all-in contracts. Finally, all-in contracts are nothing but formalized property. We want a reduction in working time, we want to limit work both in terms of content and in time. And we need to get away from the daily and weekly maximum working hours. Ladies and gentlemen! We certainly do not have an unemployment rate of 20 percent in Austria, and it is not the same with us as in large American cities that you have to work around the clock in order to be able to afford at least a small apartment. But to be able to speak of an island of the Blessed Ones, we are really miles away, because the burdens of the people on the workplace are getting bigger and bigger, especially for young people. We just need more quality of life and less pressure. We need measures to reduce actual working time. And we need measures to ensure a fairer distribution of working time. In my requests here today, there are a number of proposals on how this can be addressed, and I hope that this debate continues so that we will be presented with proposals in this direction very soon, which we can then discuss and where we can deal with joint solutions. As has already been said: People in this country don't want to go on like this!

At the time, the BZÖ did not agree to this law/novelle, because we already felt that it could be problematic for small and medium-sized enterprises and that these changes led, in particular, to a strengthening of those corporations, those shopping centres and trade chains, which, through this flexibility, also have greater opportunities at the opening times. At the time, a concession was made to workers by saying: Part-time workers receive a 25% supplement. However, it has not been understood that through this strong flexibilization – I say it again: Flexibility is basically nothing bad – too little on mental stress, which also takes into account the need to reconcile family and work. Now, when I look at myself so that a discussion about opening hours on Sunday is kind of kind, and then the arguments are: Well, this is only for those who make this voluntary, everyone can decide for themselves, there is a better payment!, then we know from the practice currently practiced that this is not always voluntary and that the better payment is actually only a reduction of overtime. ParlaMint-AT_2011-06-15-024-XXIV-NRSITZ-00109_d8e21032	Mr President. Federal Minister! Ladies and gentlemen! They are certainly also known from your environment or circle of acquaintances where workers are in fact restricted by undue competition clauses in their freedom. Unfortunately, this has developed in recent years in such a way that one for posts/positions, where one in reality is not a secret is active – that is, if you were to switch from Coca-Cola to Pepsi or vice versa – with competitive clauses in the contract of employment. It is therefore very, very important that we stop this inconsistency and ensure that there are competition clauses only where, in fact, very special company-related expertise is required. For all of us in this debate, the question of how to deal with the concept of workers in Austria in the future was also of interest. I have heard with great interest from representatives of the government parties that one is working very seriously to sum up the completely different employee concepts in Austria – workers, employees, contract staff, officials. I am surprised because today morning/early we heard about the measures discussed and decided on at Semmering. But I do not think that this measure has been mentioned here. That is, if you are actually planning this, then you can certainly also expect support from FPÖ, because we believe that this old separation, this boxing in the area of employeeship, should actually belong to the past. This common concept would also offer a great opportunity to find a uniform and just solution in the area of pension access. I would also like to make an offer to SPÖ, because it is necessary to consider seriously whether a measure should be implemented in the case of pension contributions, which guarantees that the contributions paid in the context of wages/earners may be reduced a little, and on the other hand that all personal income is used and thus also support pension funds. This would be a process that we could imagine very, very well, although this would naturally have to happen neutrally. Ladies and gentlemen, it has also been mentioned briefly today that the opposition is dissatisfied because there is so little debate on government documents today. I can say from my point of view: I am very glad that we are now working on so many opposition proposals in the Committee on Labour and Social Affairs. We will still need many meetings until some of the requests we have made in recent years have actually been dealt with. However, I think it is really necessary to deal very seriously with the concerns, requests and ideas of all the parties represented here in Parliament and to debate these ideas and requests here in plenary. I would like to make a positive contribution to this today, with the exception of the government parties, and I would like to thank the fact that we are at last prepared to debate requests here. It can be found that the remuneration of €1 300 gross per month – and it must be said that since 2001 it has been most actively worked on – is already collectively secured in the majority of the economy in which most of the employees are employed. That is why the present proposal is not in line with. On the motion for a resolution 62/A(E) I would just like to point out § 27 of the postal market law. There it is explicitly stated that the granting of a concession requires compliance with the applicable labour law provisions, including the appropriate remuneration, and that a concession is not granted without these conditions. In my opinion, these compliance requirements – and I would like to mention this – prevent the circumvention of labour law provisions and regulations relating to the concept of workers. I am thinking of § 1151 and the following of the General Civil Code. On the motion for a resolution 1196/A(E), which mentions the package against precarious employment, wage and social dumping, I would like to point out that the demand for precarious employment was already responded to in 2008. The full social security scheme has been introduced, so it can be said, with full social security for the free workers who deserve beyond the threshold. I mean, this is a very important social policy achievement. Dear ladies and gentlemen! In addition, the wage and social dumping law, which was adopted by 1. May 2011 entered into force, by law, ensuring that under-remuneration, wage dumping in Austria is not possible unpunished. This law also precisely regulates the control, the competence of control. The administrative penalty with penalties of up to €50 000 is an important instrument when it comes to under-remuneration, the fight against wage dumping. In addition, this Act ensures that the prescribed taxes and social contributions must be paid and that the working conditions as laid down in the collective agreement are respected. With § 14 of the UWG – law against unfair competition – there is also the possibility of a “verbandklag” – under quotation marks – and the social partners thus have a suitable instrument to counter wage dumping legally. Dear ladies and gentlemen! We note from the SPÖ Group that for us wage policy is collective contract policy, that it is well anchored in the social partners and, as in the past, it is made and designed successfully for employees in the future. [[Congratulation of the Abg. Dr. Bartenstein]] With the re-codification of labour contract law – which is also defined in the government programme – we achieve an approximation, a harmonisation of worker concepts, and I am confident that a common social partnership agreement will be reached. In summary, it can be found that a lot has already been done by the Ministry of Social Affairs, a lot of this issue is at work and we will therefore take note of the negative committee reports. Thank you.

Imagine, 14.7 million hours a week, these people are volunteering and free of charge. Ladies and gentlemen, the added value for society, but also the added value for each individual and for every individual, I think, is very big, especially because on the one hand solidarity is strengthened and, on the other hand, the development of personality and the development of personality is significant. The European Union, we all know, has declared 2011 the "European Year of Voluntaryness" and, in particular in the EU, but also in the individual Member States, wants to work to create a favourable framework for voluntary activity, to promote voluntary activity, to strengthen organisations, to strengthen the recognition of volunteering and to raise awareness of it, and to raise awareness of the value of volunteering in particular. Austria – also in the form of Mr Rudolf Hundstorfer – works and participates very intensively in this year. ==References== In June, a “day of honorary office ” is to give special attention to many volunteers, many volunteers, as well as to diversity. Ladies and gentlemen, the government parties have made a commitment to strengthen the voluntary commitment and, above all, to improve the legal protection of the voluntary social year. By the end of the year, such a government proposal is to be presented with a bill. We have also decided on a motion for a resolution in the Committee on Economic and Monetary Affairs and Industrial Policy. I would like to stress once more the importance of this voluntary social year, which is a special form in the field of volunteering. They all know that the voluntary social year is mainly used or exercised by young people. In particular, it is intended to enable practical knowledge of social activities. It should also be the personal clarification of the suitability in a social, health or care profession. This is all happening under a very large voluntary commitment, which, from our point of view, is without a doubt beyond the classical volunteer commitment and therefore, especially, and I would like to say, also needs to be dealt with separately throughout the whole voluntary activity. There is currently a special directive of the Federal Ministry of Labour, Social Affairs and Consumer Protection which provides compensation for young people. This special directive is intended to lead to a law by the end of the year in which social interests are considered and accounting models for vocational training et cetera are also envisaged. However, ladies and gentlemen, from the point of view of social democracy, it is particularly important that young people or people who complete the voluntary social year are not used as cheap workers, that volunteering – and I attach particular importance to it – should never replace labour market policies, at best, and that the voluntary year should remain voluntary. Mrs. Haubner, therefore, we cannot agree to your request for assistance, which you ask for or put into play, because in this request you have brought into question the mandatory use of long-term unemployed people, well-known by those who are suitable. For us voluntary activity is simply voluntary. In addition, I would also like to “warn” – under quotations – that the maintenance of the existing health and care system, just as it is in your request, is built on the foundation of the honorary office, whether by voluntary staff or by a voluntary social year. I think, even here, that the care and health system cannot be built on it, it needs particularly well-trained and well-paid professionals. In my opinion, I believe that honors can be complementary, but they can never replace them in this area either. I am convinced that honorary office and voluntary commitment have a great added value – as I have said – for society as well as for everyone. Nevertheless, and perhaps precisely because it is important for society, we are called upon, at a time of individualization, to actually promote awareness of this and, of course, to create legal frameworks. I think that the proposal we have decided to make is another step in this direction.
